Lifting shadings on symmetrically self-dual subfactor planar algebras
Zhengwei Liu, Scott Morrison, David Penneys

Abstract
In this note, we discuss the notion of symmetric self-duality of shaded planar algebras, which allows us to lift shadings on subfactor planar algebras to obtain ℤ / 2 ​ ℤ {\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}} -graded unitary fusion categories. This finishes the proof that there are unitary fusion categories with fusion graphs 4442 and 3333.



Planar algebras have proven to be useful in the construction
[
Pet10
,
BMPS12
]
and classification
[
JMS14
,
AMP15
]
of subfactors and fusion categories.
In recent articles, we used planar algebras to construct subfactor planar algebras with principal graphs 4442, 3333, and 2221
[
MP15
]
,

{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{diagrams/graphs/ACE47192690819DF}}\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt}\,,\,{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{diagrams/graphs/772136FAF425B059}}\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt}\,,\text{ and }{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{diagrams/graphs/63FDB4A04F409318}}\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt},

and a new subfactor with principal graphs 22221 with interesting dual data
[
LMP15
]
(see Example
2.2
below).
This 22221 subfactor turns out to be an example of a new parameterized family of unshaded subfactor planar algebras related to quantum subgroups
[
Liu15
]
.
The 2221 subfactor was originally constructed by Izumi
[
Izu01
]
, as was the 3333 subfactor
[
Izu16
]
.

In
[
CMS11
, Appendix]
, Ostrik constructed a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded unitary fusion category with fusion graph 2221.
Thus upon constructing 4442 and 3333, we naturally wondered whether we could lift the shading on our subfactor planar algebras to get
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded fusion categories with these fusion graphs.

We showed that these subfactor planar algebras are
symmetrically self-dual
, i.e., there is a planar algebra isomorphism
\Phi
from
{\mathcal{P}}_{\bullet}=({\mathcal{P}}_{+},{\mathcal{P}}_{-})
to its dual
\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\bullet}=({\mathcal{P}}_{-},{\mathcal{P}}_{+})
such that
\Phi_{\mp}\circ\Phi_{\pm}=\boldsymbol{1}_{\pm}
.
We furthermore claimed that we can lift the shading on a symmetrically self-dual subfactor planar algebra to obtain an unshaded factor planar algebra
[
BHP12
]
.
This would mean the associated tensor category of projections is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded unitary fusion category
{\mathcal{C}}
whose even graded part is the even part of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\bullet}
and whose odd graded part is the odd part of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\bullet}
.

In this note, we complete the proof of this claim to complete the construction of these categories.

.

Corollary B
.

There are
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded unitary fusion categories with fusion graphs 4442 and 3333.

1
Unshaded and shaded planar algebras

We refer the reader to
[
Pet10
,
Jon12
]
for the definition of a shaded subfactor planar algebra, and to
[
BHP12
,
HP17
]
for the definition of an unshaded factor planar algebra.
As we need to shade unshaded planar algebras and lift the shading on shaded planar algebras, we introduce the following notation to help keep things as simple as possible.

is obviously a symmetric self-duality, since the action
is independent of the shading, and merely depends on the unshaded action
.

Theorem
A
says that all symmetrically self-dual shaded planar algebras come from unshaded planar algebras.
This theorem is both unsurprising and relatively tedious to prove.
At this point a number of fusion categories have been discovered first in the form of shaded planar algebras (see
[
MP15
,
LMP15
]
).
The motivation of this article is to complete the construction of these categories.

To prove Theorem
A
, we introduce the following notation.

Notation 1.5
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{T}}
has
t
input disks.
For
1\leq i\leq t
, we let
D_{i}({\mathcal{T}})
denote the
i
-th input disk of
{\mathcal{T}}
, and we let
D_{0}({\mathcal{T}})
denote the output disk of
{\mathcal{T}}
.
We write
k_{i}({\mathcal{T}})
to denote the number of boundary points of
D_{i}({\mathcal{T}})
.
Finally, if
{\mathcal{T}}
is shaded, we write
\pm_{i}({\mathcal{T}})
to denote whether the distinguished interval of the disk
D_{i}({\mathcal{T}})
is in an unshaded or shaded region respectively.

Definition 1.6
.

For an unshaded planar tangle
whose input and output disks all have an even number of boundary points, we define a shaded tangle

Remark 1.9
.

Definition
1.4
and Theorem
A
show that there is a bijective correspondence between symmetric self-dualities on subfactor planar algebras and unshaded
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded factor planar algebras.
This gives one way to construct examples of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded extensions of the principal even half
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
of a finite depth subfactor planar algebra by its odd half
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
.

The complete answer to this question was given by
[
GJS15
]
, using the techniques of
[
ENO10
]
.
In particular,
[
GJS15
]
gives a cohomological condition under which any invertible self-dual
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
-bimodule category
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
gives exactly 2 such extensions of the form
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\oplus{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
.

It is possible there might be extensions of the form
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\oplus{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
which are not generated by symmetrically self-dual objects.
However, we have no such examples at this time.

2
Examples

Example 2.1
.

In
[
MP15
]
, we showed that several spoke subfactors are symmetrically self-dual, in particular, the 4442, 3333 (
3^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}}
), and 2221 subfactors with principal graphs

{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{diagrams/graphs/ACE47192690819DF}}\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt}\,,\,{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{diagrams/graphs/772136FAF425B059}}\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt}\,,\text{ and }{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{diagrams/graphs/63FDB4A04F409318}}\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt}\,.

Example 2.2
.

Many braided subfactor planar algebras coming from BMW algebras
[
Wen88
,
BJL17
]
are really unshaded.
In
[
LMP15
]
, we introduced a notion of
\sigma
-braided subfactor planar algebras, and we showed our examples with principal graphs

{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{diagrams/graphs/FF05E76A92AAD72C}}\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt}\text{ and }{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{diagrams/graphs/70D1C9A600E435C3}}\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt}

are symmetrically self-dual.
The former one is a special case of BMW planar algebras and
\sigma=\pm 1
. The latter one is new and
\sigma=\pm i
.
Their
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
fixed point planar algebras have principle graphs
2^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}1
and
2^{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}1
respectively (22221).

Example 2.3
.

The
\sigma=\pm i
subfactor in Example
2.2
turns out to be a special case of a new parameterized family of unshaded subfactor planar algebras
[
Liu15
]
related to quantum subgroups.

Example 2.4
.

For a finite group
G
, the group subfactor
R\subset R\rtimes G
is self-dual if and only if
G
is abelian.
For
G
abelian, the group subfactor
R\subset R\rtimes G
has many symmetric self-dualities, which we parametrize in §
3
below via symmetric bicharacters.
The resulting unitary fusion categories obtained via Theorem
A
are Tambara-Yamagami categories
[
TY98
]
.

Recall that given an abelian group
A
, a non-degenerate symmetric bicharacter
\chi:A\times A\to S^{1}
, and a sign
\pm
, the Tambara-Yamagami category
{\mathcal{T}}{\mathcal{Y}}(A,\chi,\pm)
is a skeletal category with objects
a\in A
and an object
m
satisfying the following fusion rules:

(1)

a\otimes b=ab
for all
a,b\in A

(2)

a\otimes m=m\otimes a=m
for all
a\in A
, and

(3)

m\otimes m=\bigoplus_{a\in A}a
.

It was shown in
[
TY98
]
that the categories
{\mathcal{T}}{\mathcal{Y}}(A,\chi,\pm)
and
{\mathcal{T}}{\mathcal{Y}}(A^{\prime},\chi^{\prime},\pm^{\prime})
are equivalent if and only if there is an isomorphism
A\to A^{\prime}
which sends
\chi\to\chi^{\prime}
, and the signs are equal:
\pm=\pm^{\prime}
.
(See also
[
ENO10
, Example 9.4]
.)

For example, Tambara-Yamagami showed in
[
TY98
, Theorem 4.1]
that there are only two inequivalent symmetric bicharacters on
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
, which gives 4 inequivalent categories.
Similarly, it is straightforward to show that there are only 2 inequivalent symmetric bicharacters on
{\mathbb{Z}}/4{\mathbb{Z}}
, which gives 4 inequivalent categories.

By
[
GHR13
]
,
all Tambara-Yamagami categories are unitary,
since they are weakly group theoretical.
However, to get a factor planar algebra, we need that
m
is symmetrically self-dual, i.e.,
m
has Frobenius-Schur indicator 1
[
NS07
]
.
The Frobenius-Schur indicators for Tambara-Yamagami categories were completely worked out in
[
Shi11
]
, where it was shown that
\nu_{2}(a)=\delta_{a^{2},e}
and
\nu_{2}(m)=\pm
, the sign in
{\mathcal{T}}{\mathcal{Y}}(A,\chi,\pm)
.
Hence we must have
\pm=+
to get a factor planar algebra.

Example 2.5
.

The
m
-interval Jones-Wassermann subfactors for modular tensor categories are symmetrically self-dual for all
m\geq 1

[
LX16
]
.
This result was inspired by the open problem to construct a conformal net whose representation category is a prescribed unitary modular tensor category.
We refer the reader to
[
LX16
]
for further references related to Jones-Wassermann subfactors of conformal nets.

In particular, if we start with a unitary modular tensor category whose fusion ring is a finite abelian group
A
, then its
m
-interval Jones-Wassermann subfactor, for any
m\geq 2
, gives a symmetrically self-dual group subfactor (with group
A
), as well as a Tambara-Yamagami category as in Example
2.4
.
Moreover, the symmetric bicharacter
\chi
of
A
is determined by the modular
S
matrix of the modular tensor category.

3

Given an abelian group
A
, we obtain a group subfactor planar algebra

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{A}_{\bullet}
.
The minimal projections of
\hbox to7.17pt{\vbox to8.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.78171pt\lower-5.94827pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{A}_{\bullet}\to\overline{\hbox to7.17pt{\vbox to8.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.78171pt\lower-5.94827pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{A}}_{\bullet}
is a shaded planar algebra
*
-isomorphism, then the coefficients
\chi(g,h)
defined above give a bicharacter on
A
.

Proof.

We define
\Phi_{\pm}
as in the statement and extend the map linearly to labelled tangles in the universal planar algebra generated by 2-boxes.
By
[
Liu16
, Theorem 2.26]
, it is enough to prove that
\Phi_{-}
preserves the adjoint operator, the trace, the contragredient, the multiplication, and the coproduct on 2-boxes.

Step 1:

the extension
\Phi_{-}
preserves the adjoint operator:

(\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g})))^{*}=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d}\overline{\chi(g,h)}P_{h}=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d}\chi(g^{-1},h)P_{h}=\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g^{-1}})=\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}(P_{g})=(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g}))^{*}.

Step 2:

the extension
\Phi_{-}
preserves the trace:

\Tr(\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g})))=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d}\chi(g,h)=d\delta_{g=e}=\Tr(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g})),

where
\delta_{g=e}
is the Kronecker function, and
e
is the identity of the group
A
.

Step 3:

the extension
\Phi_{-}
preserves the contragredient:

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g}))}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d}\chi(g,h)\overline{P_{h}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d}\chi(g,h)P_{h^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d}\chi(g,h^{-1})P_{h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d}\overline{\chi(g,h)}P_{h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d}\chi(g^{-1},h)P_{h}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g^{-1}})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}_{S}(\overline{P_{g}}).

Step 4:

the extension
\Phi_{-}
preserves the multiplication:

\displaystyle\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{1}}))\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{2}}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\chi(g_{1},h)\chi(g_{2},h)P_{h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in A}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\chi(g_{1}g_{2},h)P_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{1}g_{2}}))

\displaystyle=\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{1}}*P_{g_{2}}))

\displaystyle=\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{1}})\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{2}})).

Step 5:

the extension
\Phi_{-}
preserves the coproduct:

\displaystyle\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{1}}))*\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{2}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{h_{1},h_{2}\in A}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\chi(g_{1},h_{1})\chi(g_{2},h_{2})P_{h_{1}}*P_{h_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h_{1},h\in A}\frac{1}{d^{3}}\chi(g_{1},h_{1})\chi(g_{2},h_{1}^{-1}h)P_{h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h_{1},h\in A}\frac{1}{d^{3}}\chi(g_{1}g_{2}^{-1},h_{1})\chi(g_{2},h)P_{h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h_{1}\in A}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\chi(g_{1}g_{2}^{-1},h_{1})\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{2}})

\displaystyle=\delta_{g_{1},g_{2}}\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{1}}P_{g_{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{1}})*\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{2}}).

Therefore
\Phi
extends uniquely to a shaded planar algebra
*
-isomorphism
\Phi:\hbox to7.17pt{\vbox to8.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.78171pt\lower-5.94827pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

.

\Phi:\hbox to7.17pt{\vbox to8.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.78171pt\lower-5.94827pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.76 1.18 L -1.38 1.18 C -0.62 1.18 -0 0.65 -0 0 C -0 -0.65 -0.62 -1.18 -1.38 -1.18 L -2.76 -1.18 L -2.76 1.77}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{A}}_{\bullet}
is a shaded planar algebra
*
-isomorphism, then by definition,

\chi(g,h)=d\Tr(\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g}))P_{h}).

In particular
\chi(e,h)=\Tr(P_{h})=1
. By the computation in Step 3,

\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{1}}))\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{2}}))=\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{1}})\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g_{2}}))

implies that

\chi(g_{1}g_{2},h)=\chi(g_{1},h)\chi(g_{2},h).

Thus
\chi(g,h)
is a character with respect to
g
.
On the other hand,

\chi(g,h)=d\Tr(\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g}))P_{h})=d\Tr(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g})\Phi_{-}^{-1}(P_{h}))

and in particular
\chi(g,e)=d\Tr(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g})P_{e})=1
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\chi(g,h_{1}h_{2})

\displaystyle=d\Tr(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g})\Phi_{-}^{-1}(P_{h_{1}h_{2}}))

\displaystyle=d\Tr(P_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}(\Phi_{-}^{-1}(P_{h_{1}h_{2}})))

\displaystyle=d^{2}\Tr(P_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}(\Phi_{-}^{-1}(P_{h_{1}}*P_{h_{2}})))

\displaystyle=d^{2}\Tr(P_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}((\Phi_{-}^{-1}(P_{h_{1}})*\Phi^{-1}(P_{h_{2}})))

\displaystyle=d^{2}\Tr(P_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}(\Phi_{-}^{-1}(P_{h_{1}}))\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}(\Phi^{-1}(P_{h_{2}})))

\displaystyle=d^{2}\Tr(P_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}(\Phi_{-}^{-1}(P_{h_{1}})))\Tr(P_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}(\Phi^{-1}(P_{h_{2}})))

\displaystyle=\chi(g,h_{1})\chi(g,h_{2}).

Therefore
\chi(g,h)
is a bicharacter.
∎

Theorem 3.2
.

The bicharacter
\chi(g,h)
in Theorem
3.1
is symmetric if and only if
\Phi^{2}=1
, namely
\hbox to7.17pt{\vbox to8.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.78171pt\lower-5.94827pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.51 -7.68 L -5.51 3.54 L -1.38 3.54 C 0.91 3.54 2.76 1.96 2.76 -0 C 2.76 -1.96 0.91 -3.54 -1.38 -3.54 L -2.76 -3.54 L -2.76 -7.68 L -6.06 -7.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.76 1.18 L -1.38 1.18 C -0.62 1.18 -0 0.65 -0 0 C -0 -0.65 -0.62 -1.18 -1.38 -1.18 L -2.76 -1.18 L -2.76 1.77}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{A}_{\bullet}
is symmetrically self-dual.

Proof.

Note that
\Phi_{+}\Phi_{-}=\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}\Phi_{-}\mathfrak{F}_{S}\Phi_{-}
, so
\Phi^{2}=1
if and only if
(\Phi_{-}\mathfrak{F}_{S})^{2}=\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{2}
.

If the bicharacter
\chi(g,h)
is symmetric, then

(\Phi_{-}\mathfrak{F}_{S})^{2}(P_{g})=\sum_{h,k\in A}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\chi(h,k)\chi(g,h)P_{k}=\sum_{h,k\in A}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\chi(g+k,h)P_{k}=P_{g^{-1}}=\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{2}(P_{g}).

Thus
(\Phi_{-}\mathfrak{F}_{S})^{2}=\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{2}
and
\Phi^{2}=1
.

On the other hand, if
\Phi^{2}=1
, then

\displaystyle\chi(g,h)

\displaystyle=d\Tr(\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g}))P_{h})

\displaystyle=d\Tr(\mathfrak{F}_{S}^{-1}(\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{g})))\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{h}))

\displaystyle=d\Tr(\Phi_{-}^{-1}(P_{g})\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{h}))

\displaystyle=d\Tr(P_{g}\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{h})))

\displaystyle=d\Tr(\Phi_{-}(\mathfrak{F}_{S}(P_{h}))P_{g})

\displaystyle=\chi(h,g),

and the bicharacter is symmetric.
∎

For a finite abelian group
A
and a non-degenerate symmetric bicharacter
\chi:A\times A\to S^{1}
,
the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded unitary fusion category corresponding to the unshaded planar algebra
\hbox to7.17pt{\vbox to8.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.78171pt\lower-5.94827pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.51 -7.68 L -5.51 3.54 L -1.38 3.54 C 0.91 3.54 2.76 1.96 2.76 -0 C 2.76 -1.96 0.91 -3.54 -1.38 -3.54 L -2.76 -3.54 L -2.76 -7.68 L -6.06 -7.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.76 1.18 L -1.38 1.18 C -0.62 1.18 -0 0.65 -0 0 C -0 -0.65 -0.62 -1.18 -1.38 -1.18 L -2.76 -1.18 L -2.76 1.77}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{A}_{\bullet}
is the Tambara-Yamagami category.
We refer the readers to
[
LX16
]
for a more general case, where
A
is replaced by a unitary modular tensor category and
\chi
is replaced by the modular
S
matrix.
